## 2026-03-07 — web round (Rosicky goose keeper; anti-shame restitution lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane around hidden shame, restitution, and hospitality to the compromised rather than returning to the more exhausted saint / beggar / Plough shelves.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Goose Bought Back Before Dawn.”** Source lane: Willa Cather’s **“Neighbour Rosicky.”** What cleared the bar was the double mercy: the young man is not excused in his theft, but strangers refuse to leave him either hungry or trapped in his shame. They feed him, hear the truth, and put enough back into his hands for restitution plus abundance. That feels close to Jesus’ way of opening a road home without pretending the wrong was nothing.
- Strong near-miss: Sarah Orne Jewett’s **“The Town Poor.”** Morally excellent on restoring dignity instead of merely maintaining pauper status, but more social sketch than portable parable image; I left it off the main shelf for now.
- Secondary near-miss: the **Jane Eyre / Moor House** rescue chapter. Beautiful anti-shame hospitality—the sisters wash the stranger’s clothes while she sleeps so she can come down clean—but still too novelistic and scene-dependent for this shelf’s cadence.
- Freeman comparison lane: **“The Cat”** was genuinely strange and beautiful (a starving outcast sharing half a rabbit with a cat, then being hosted all winter by the cat’s providence), but the animal-centered frame felt less Jesus-like than Rosicky’s human road from hunger to restitution. **“The Gospel According to Joan,” “The Christmas Ghost,”** and **“A Burglar’s Christmas”** were useful boundary checks, but either too Christmas-sentimental or too close to existing prodigal / grace pieces already on the shelf.
- **No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.** The found story was cleaner than anything original I considered.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (A Letter to God kept)
Kept / added to Parables.txt:
- “The Letter Sent Through Other Hands,” retold from Gregorio López y Fuentes’s “A Letter to God.”
  Why it cleared the bar: the kindness is anonymous, practical, and willing to go misunderstood. The giver protects hope without insisting on recognition or gratitude.

Serious near-misses:
- O. Henry, “A Retrieved Reformation” — strong self-sacrifice and mercy, but the center of gravity felt a little too romantic/sentimental for this shelf.
- Dostoevsky, “An Honest Thief” — deeply compassionate, but more tragic character sketch than clean parable image.

Fresh lane notes:
- Rejected or merely probed: Dickens’s “Seven Poor Travellers,” various poverty/charity anthologies, and a handful of sacred-text/folktale indexes. Most failed because they explained virtue too directly or lacked a single memorable mercy-image.


## 2026-03-07 — web round (duplicate found; no new keeper)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane around hidden mercy from socially low-status figures, courtroom shame, and inner repentance rather than miracle/reward mechanics.
- **No new keeper added this round.**
- Most important find: Anton Chekhov’s **“The Beggar”** initially looked like a live candidate because the real mercy belongs not to the lecturing lawyer but to the cook, who chops the wood herself and weeps over the drunkard until he changes. But this turned out to be a duplicate in substance: the archive already contains it as **“The Woman Who Chopped the Wood.”** Useful control discovery; do not revisit as a fresh candidate.
- Strong near-miss: Selma Lagerlöf’s **“The Girl from the Marsh Croft.”** Powerful image—a disgraced young mother stops the father of her child from perjuring himself in court because she still loves him and fears for his soul. Real moral beauty, but it feels more like sacrificial romantic drama / legal scene than a portable Jesus-grade parable image.
- Secondary near-miss: Robert Louis Stevenson’s **“Markheim.”** Strong repentance turn, but too interior, metaphysical, and devil-dialogue driven for this shelf.
- Minor boundary checks: Selma Lagerlöf’s **Christ Legends** and “That Little Beggar” pages were worth a glance but yielded nothing cleaner than the duplicate Chekhov lane.
- Heuristic sharpened: beware candidates whose force comes mainly from courtroom theater, supernatural conversation, or a final explanation. The shelf still seems to favor ordinary hidden mercy that changes a person before anyone names it.
- **No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.** Nothing original I considered felt cleaner than an honest empty turn.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Gissing / humane public-domain lane / no keeper)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh lane of public-domain humane stories and anthologies that had not yet been mined here: Gissing, Stowe, Wilde, Andersen, Plough list pages, and a didactic story-anthology check.
- **No new keeper added this round.**
- Strongest near-miss: George Gissing’s **“The Salt of the Earth.”** There is real beauty in the ordinary clerk whose kindness keeps being quietly spent away by other people. But the story’s force lies more in the pathos of exploitation than in one clean image of mercy that can travel like a parable.
- Secondary near-miss: Gissing’s **“A Poor Gentleman.”** Morally serious and anti-shame in places, but it turns on embarrassment, self-concealment, and class-decorum rather than an image of mercy vivid enough for the shelf.
- Boundary checks declined quickly: Wilde’s **“The Model Millionaire”** (reward-logic too neat); **“The Happy Prince”** (beautiful but too allegorical / magical); Andersen’s **“The Little Match-Seller”** (piercing but more martyr-vision than neighbor-mercy); Stowe’s **“The Widow’s Bandbox”** (good-hearted but baggy, comic, and more proverb than parable); Plough’s **“Willibald’s Trip to Heaven”** (too courtroom-afterlife / accusatory); and **World Stories Retold** (too pedagogical / pre-selected for moral instruction).
- Heuristic sharpened again: this shelf keeps rejecting stories where goodness is rewarded from above, exposed in a courtroom, or dissolved into allegory. The cleanest lane is still hidden, ordinary mercy that restores dignity without trying to look exemplary.
- **No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.** Nothing original I considered felt rarer or cleaner than an honest empty turn.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Montgomery / O. Henry / anthology / dignity-without-spectacle lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh lane around public-domain humane stories and anthology shelves not yet logged here: Lucy Maud Montgomery, O. Henry boundary pieces, and a couple of generic short-story repositories only to rule them out.
- **No new keeper added this round.**
- Strong near-miss: Lucy Maud Montgomery’s **“A Golden Wedding.”** The image is lovely: a man who was once taken in as a troublesome orphan spends nearly all he has to bring an old couple home from the poorhouse and restore their furniture before their anniversary. I left it off the shelf because the emotional force leans too much on gratitude-return and community celebration; it feels more like beautiful repayment than the freer mercy-image this archive tends to keep.
- Strong near-miss: O. Henry’s **“Two Thanksgiving Day Gentlemen.”** Rich in dignity and pathos, especially in the old man who has gone hungry to preserve a yearly ritual of feeding one poor stranger. But the story is built on irony and mutual burden more than liberating mercy; both men are trapped by the tradition rather than set free by it.
- Secondary near-miss: O. Henry’s **“An Unfinished Story.”** The line about a furnished room being the place where “other people do not know it when you go hungry” is piercing, but the piece remains social observation and danger-sketch rather than a portable parable image.
- Quick boundary checks declined: Dostoevsky’s **“Poor People”** (too epistolary and diffuse), H. G. Wells’s **“The Apple”** (too strange/fantastic), and the On Being repost of **“The Little Monk and the Samurai”** (too famous, too already-koan-shaped, and not the right moral grain for this shelf).
- Heuristic sharpened again: keep preferring stories where mercy interrupts shame without turning into repayment, ritual obligation, or a clever twist.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Blue Umbrella keeper; fresh Indian-literature comparison lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a genuinely fresh lane rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Chekhov / Premchand / Hughes / Plough shelves.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Umbrella Left Open.”** Source lane: Ruskin Bond’s **The Blue Umbrella.** What cleared the bar was the final release: the girl sees what envy has done to the old shopkeeper, and when the coveted thing finally comes into his hands he discovers it is useless if held only for himself. She lets him keep it, and the umbrella ends by standing open for anyone on the road to borrow. That movement—from envy, to relinquishment, to common shelter—felt rare and clean enough for the shelf.
- Boundary checks this round mostly confirmed the choice rather than overtaking it. The Basheer / Narayan lane surfaced humane material, but none of it beat the umbrella image for portability and mercy-without-sermon.
- Useful caution sharpened again: the shelf tends to prefer stories where an ordinary object becomes morally radiant not by magic or reward, but because someone stops clenching it.
- **No addition to `AI-Parables.txt` this round.** The found story was cleaner than anything original I considered.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Chekhov / T.S. Arthur / Japanese-folklore / public-domain humane sweep; no keeper)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked several genuinely fresh lanes rather than returning to the already exhausted Tolstoy / Premchand / Plough / Hughes shelves.
- **No new keeper added this round.**
- Strong near-miss: Anton Chekhov’s **“At Christmas Time.”** It has real ache and one clean image—the old parents paying a village scribbler to write to the daughter they have not heard from in years—but the story remains more loneliness-and-distance than mercy enacted. Beautiful, not shelf-ready.
- Strong near-miss: T. S. Arthur’s **“An Angel in Disguise.”** The rough movement is morally appealing: a hard woman takes in a bedridden child and is changed by caring for her. I left it out because the title and closing frame do too much of the moral work, and the child is turned too directly into a sanctifying instrument.

- Boundary check rejected: Gösta Berling’s **“The Beggar”** episode. There is a fierce image there—the child sending the beggar away with the stolen meal, then hunting him with a lantern in the snow—but the whole scene leans toward intoxication, melodrama, and later rescue rather than a plain portable mercy-image.
- General pattern this round: many fresh public-domain stories were humane, but they either sermonized, rewarded virtue too visibly, or converted the vulnerable into props for another person’s improvement.

- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh lane built around dignity-of-the-poor scenes, stranger hospitality, and saint-adjacent literary stories that had not yet been logged here.

- Strong near-miss: Jacob Riis, **The Children of the Poor**. The Sabbath-table image is beautiful: a nearly empty household receives a guest with royal dignity. But the force comes as reportage and social witness rather than as a self-contained parable-shape, so I left it off the shelf.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Jewett / The Town Poor keeper)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane centered on Sarah Orne Jewett and neighboring humane stories rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Chekhov / Plough / Premchand lanes.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Last Peach in the Attic.”** Source lane: Sarah Orne Jewett’s **“The Town Poor.”** What cleared the bar was not merely that the poor are visited or rescued, but that while lodged in a freezing attic with almost nothing left, the Bray sisters still enact hospitality: cold tea, crackers, and the last preserved peach are given to their guests without self-pity. The turn that made it shelf-worthy was even sharper: Ann Bray wants their neglectful hostess Mrs. Janes invited next time too, because she has also had a hard life. That movement—from deprivation, to dignity, to pity for the person failing them—felt very rare.
- Strong near-miss: Louisa May Alcott’s **“Little Button-Rose.”** Pleasant and morally decent, but too school-reader / sweetness-forward, with peacemaking carried by charm rather than by a severe mercy-image.
- Boundary checks declined: Sarah Orne Jewett’s **“Miss Tempy’s Watchers,” “Martha’s Lady,” “Aunt Cynthy Dallett,”** and related commentary lanes. Interesting, but either more about ritual/reconciliation, longing, or social charm than a portable Jesus-grade parable image.
- Heuristic sharpened again: one reliable sign is when the poor are not merely recipients of pity but remain spiritually spacious enough to offer welcome, sweetness, or compassion to others while still wronged.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (French Christmas anthology / duplicate-check on Thank You, Ma’am / no keeper)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane through French/Spanish Christmas-story anthologies, German/French short-story collections, and one duplicate-check lane around Langston Hughes rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Jewett / Premchand / Plough shelves.
- **No new keeper added this round.**
- Strong duplicate rather than keeper: Langston Hughes’s **“Thank You, Ma’am.”** On its own it is very close to the shelf’s standard—concrete mercy, guarded dignity, trust offered before repentance is complete—but this image is already represented in the archive as **“The Purse and the Blue Shoes,”** so I left `Parables.txt` unchanged.
- Strong near-miss: Armando Palacio Valdés’s **“A Bird in the Snow.”** The blind musician collapsing in the snow and being found by his long-absent brother has genuine tenderness, but the final reunion arrives with too much providential / reward-like machinery to feel portable in the severe way this shelf prefers.
- Strong near-miss: François Coppée’s **“The Louis-d’or.”** The dreamed image of the sleeping child with the gold coin in her shoe is piercing, and the story does wake a gambler into repentance, but the nightmare frame and the moral recoil are too overtly arranged; the mercy-image does not stand free enough on its own.
- Boundary checks declined: André Theuriet’s **“A Christmas in the Forest”** (beautiful nativity atmosphere and a shepherd bringing a goat for a newborn, but too consciously biblical in shape), Alphonse Daudet’s **“Salvette and Bernadou”** (moving friendship, but more pathos than parable), Dickens’s **“The Seven Poor Travellers”** (sharp critique of institutional charity, but too essay-humorous / social-satiric), and the anthology lanes **Short-story Masterpieces** / **The Broken Cup** / **A Teacher’s Gift** / **The Sketch Book** as mostly controls rather than live contenders.
- Heuristic sharpened again: this shelf still prefers a single act of mercy that changes the moral air without relying on dream, miracle, reunion, or explicit symbolic staging.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-07 — web round (Gaskell / Cranford keeper; Chekhov boundary check)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then worked a fresh lane around Gaskell / Cranford and one Chekhov dignity-and-hunger check rather than returning to the more exhausted Tolstoy / Jewett / Premchand / Plough shelves.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Tea Bought Twice.”** Source lane: Elizabeth Gaskell’s **_Cranford_**, especially the bank-failure / tea-shop chapters. What cleared the bar was the form mercy takes: Miss Matty is not merely handed relief, but given a way to live without public humiliation. The hidden sign, the grocer sending customers instead of guarding his own trade, the townswomen buying more tea than they need, and the friends quietly paying part of the rent all protect dignity rather than staging benevolence.
- Strong near-miss: Chekhov’s **“A Nightmare.”** Morally serious and moving: the layman despises the shabby priest until he learns the priest is starving while supporting others and hiding the household’s poverty out of shame. I left it off the shelf because the story lands more as exposure-and-awakening than as one portable mercy-image.
- Heuristic sharpened again: one of the clearest shelf-signs is when kindness does not merely relieve need but spares the needy the wound of being turned into a spectacle of need.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged this round.

- Strong near-miss: Selma Lagerlöf’s Berg Rese / Tord material (via anthology excerpt). Vivid and morally charged, but too large, tragic, and salvation-dramatic; the parable-weight is dispersed through a whole narrative arc.
- Fresh heuristic reinforced: the shelf remains hardest on stories that are morally admirable yet not portable. What clears the bar is not simply kindness or repentance, but a single memorable act whose mercy can stand free of reward, miracle, court scene, legend machinery, or novel-scale consequence.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. I considered forcing an original, but nothing new felt cleaner than an honest empty turn.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (USC noble thief / Aziz Nesin / Bessie Head / Maalik ibn Deenaar; no keeper)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into a fresh lane built less around “parable” labels and more around folklore archives, African short fiction, satire, and saint-conversion anecdotes.
- **No new keeper added to `Parables.txt` this round.**
- Strongest borderline candidate: **“Noble Thief”** (USC Digital Folklore Archives). A repentant thief shelters with a starving mother, sees her boil a stone to quiet her hungry children, steals food for them, and then finds fruit on the priest’s sign-tree. It is morally alive, but I declined it because the mercy is tangled with theft and then sealed by miracle/reward machinery. It does not stay severe and portable enough once stripped down.
- Humane but declined: Aziz Nesin’s **“The House on the Border.”** The hosts repeatedly return from indifferent authorities to check that the bound thief has eaten and had water, and eventually dine with him; but the story works as bureaucratic satire and absurd social comedy more than as one clean Jesus-grade mercy-image.
- Moving but declined: Bessie Head’s **“Village People.”** The old woman who collapses from hunger and the shy pail-of-water thank-you carry real dignity, yet the piece is essay-like and diffuse. It enlarges moral atmosphere more than it gives one portable act.
- Devotional but declined: the Maalik ibn Deenaar / **“True Treasure”** anecdote. Kind manners, yes, but the center of gravity is spiritual redirection rather than neighbor-mercy, and this shelf usually asks for a human act of kindness that would still pierce even if the doctrinal framing were removed.
- Noise note: an exact-phrase lane around “thief / grandmother / cooks” surfaced obvious social-media copypasta rather than trustworthy tradition or literature; logged a representative URL and moved on.
- `AI-Parables.txt` unchanged. I considered forcing an original around hidden hospitality, but nothing I drafted felt cleaner than an honest empty turn.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (doctor forgives debts / modern-grace lane)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed away from sermonized “parable” pages into modern-grace anecdotes, literary short fiction, and a few dignity-preserving news stories.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Card That Closed the Ledger.”** Source lane: Dr. Omar Atiq forgiving former cancer patients’ unpaid balances when closing his clinic. What cleared the bar was the shape of the mercy: not applause, not reward, not a sentimental scene, but a burden quietly removed from people already suffering.
- Why it felt admissible: the act does not merely heal bodies and then bill the weak for the privilege; it refuses to let healing become a second wound. The Christmas card arriving where a collection notice might have arrived gives it a clean, portable image.
- Serious near-misses / not kept:
  - **Lynne Sharon Schwartz, “The Golden Rule.”** Morally real and serious about inconvenient kindness, but too interior and novelistic to become one clean traveling image.
  - **Lou Williams buying a meal for the would-be robber.** Vivid and disarming, but still tangled in celebrity context and anecdotal texture rather than archetypal clarity.
  - **The holiday-lights-between-houses neighborhood story.** Beautiful communal encouragement, but more atmosphere than parable-turn.
  - **The woman paying for a stranger’s birthday cake in memory of her son.** Tender, but too dependent on private grief and one-off sentiment to carry like a proverb in story form.
- Repeated-noise note: the ServiceSpace 2021 roundup kept resurfacing many adjacent acts of kindness, but most were admirable incidents rather than rare keeper-grade parables.
- `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged this round; the debt-forgiveness image was cleaner than the original parables I considered.


## 2026-03-08 — web round (pharmacy blessing / service-for-surgery / debt-abolition scans)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first and deliberately searched beyond the already-logged sermon/devotional lane; this round leaned on reportage and first-person/feature sources that preserved a single strong image.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Folded Bills at the Pharmacy.”** Source lane: Hody Childress quietly leaving cash at Geraldine Drugs each month so neighbors who came up short could still leave with their medicine.
- Why it cleared the bar: it is anonymous, bodily mercy from someone who was not wealthy; it protects dignity at the exact point where poverty and illness meet; and the image is portable enough to travel without the original names.
- The phrase about telling recipients it was “a blessing from the Lord” mattered because it let the gift arrive without the donor standing inside it. That anonymity felt closer to the collection’s center than public generosity stories.
- Serious near-misses / not kept:
  - **Demetrio Aguila’s surgery-for-volunteer-hours program.** Morally serious and genuinely creative, but still too structured by exchange/accounting to feel as clean as a parable of grace.
  - **Casey McIntyre’s dying wish to abolish others’ medical debt.** Beautiful and large-hearted, but it diffuses into campaign scale and public memorial rather than one crisp scene that can be retold in a few sentences.
  - **André Alexis, “Our Lady of Mercy.”** Literary interest only; not the right moral shape for this collection.
- `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged this round; the human-found pharmacy story was cleaner than the original parables I considered.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (Rutba / enemy-mercy / hidden hospitality / literary scans)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into new lanes: kindness-reportage, obscure literary scans, DailyGood/ServiceSpace, and finally enemy-mercy / wartime hospitality.
- **Kept and added to `Parables.txt`: “The Clinic After the Bombing.”** Source lane: the Rutba story, where Iraqi townspeople and medical workers treated injured U.S. peacemakers in 2003 even though the town hospital had just been bombed by U.S. forces. Multiple sources converged on the same core image: they carried the wounded in, stitched them with almost nothing, refused payment, and asked only that the story be told.
- Why it cleared the bar: the mercy is not abstract reconciliation after distance has cooled the wound; it happens while the smoke is still in the air. The wounded are not first sorted by nationality, guilt, tribe, or usefulness. They are seen simply as injured people in need of help.
- The strongest moral turn was not merely “enemy love” in theory but the refusal to reduce the sufferers to representatives of the power that had harmed the town. That feels close to the collection’s center and distinct enough from direct Gospel retellings to stand on its own.
- Serious near-misses / not kept:
  - Guardian kindness-of-strangers lane (groceries, airport tax, hand-holding, jacket discount, etc.): humane and often lovely, but most remained anecdotal rather than portable parable-images.
  - DailyGood / ServiceSpace lanes: rich in moral texture, but many entries expanded into commentary, inspiration, or movement-language instead of one clean act.
  - Older literary / folklore scans this round produced mostly atmosphere, doctrine, or trickster material rather than a single memorable mercy-turn.
- `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged this round; the human-found Rutba story was stronger than any original I conceived.

## Mar 8, 2026 (continued — round 14)
- Referred to `Already-Searched-URLs.txt` first, then pushed into fresh literary/essay territory rather than headline kindness roundups: *The Sun* readers’ pages, River Teeth micro-essays, and a few Guernica / memoir surfaces.
- **Kept (added to `Parables.txt`):**
  - “The Cat Food on the Step” (*The Sun*, “Acts of Kindness”) — a passerby notices that the elderly woman who daily feeds the feral cats under her house has vanished from her routine; the untaken bag of cat food becomes a summons to keep checking until help is called and the woman is found alive after a stroke.
- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The mercy is indirect and morally exact. No one sets out to perform a grand rescue. A woman’s ordinary faithfulness to small hungry creatures trains another person to notice when she herself is missing.
  - The final turn is especially strong: the rescuer understands that she was not the first giver in the story. The old woman’s unnoticed mercy had already gone out into the world and come back as watchfulness.
  - It survives retelling cleanly in under ten sentences and does not need miracle, transaction, or public applause.
- Serious near-misses / not kept:
  - the starving boy in Calcutta offering the last bite of his only hot meal of the week — piercing, but too close to other hunger/generosity images already in the archive;
  - the man on the subway giving his heavy coat and scarf to a cold woman — beautiful, but too familiar and too direct to feel rare;
  - several River Teeth and *The Sun* memoir pieces carried tenderness and moral intelligence but stayed memoir-shaped rather than condensing into one portable parable-turn.
- `AI-Parables.txt` intentionally left unchanged. The human-found story was cleaner than the originals I considered this round.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (bus became a carriage / StoryCorps kindness sweep)
- Fresh lane: StoryCorps kindness archive and podcast cluster, plus a small literary/memoir probe to avoid overfitting to Guardian reports.

- Why it cleared the bar:
  - The mercy is not rescue-from-danger or bill-paying, but dignity restored at the point where confusion could have made someone feel foolish or burdensome.

Closest candidates considered:
- **“The neighbors and the salt”** (unknown / social-media-circulating; LinkedIn mirror): morally excellent because it protects the poor from shame by letting them feel needed too. Rejected for now because provenance is thin and the collection already has several stronger dignity-preserving stories in this same moral lane.
- **Shabari’s tasted berries** (various Hindu retellings): beautiful “love over form” / “sincerity over ritual” energy, and very compatible with Jesus’ heart. Rejected this round because it overlaps too much with existing entries like Moses-and-the-shepherd / alphabet-prayer / heart-over-performance stories.
- **Man hiring the poor to move stacks of wood from porch to back and back again** (Aish, “Giving With Dignity”): clean and strong, but more anecdotal illustration than full parable-shape; also quite close to our existing hidden-charity / dignity-protecting material.
- **Gershon Burd hidden-chesed piece**: admirable life, but biography rather than parable.
- **Cab ride / final ride to hospice** surfaced again from fresh mirrors and fact-check pages; clearly already represented in collection (“The Unmetered Ride”), so no need to re-add.

Heuristic check:
- Good enough to keep requires more than “heartwarming.”
- I’m looking for a story that (1) protects dignity or reveals mercy, (2) turns the hearer inward, (3) can survive being retold in a few sentences without collapsing into slogan, and (4) does not merely duplicate a stronger piece already in the archive.

Action taken:
- Logged newly surfaced URLs into Already-Searched-URLs.txt.
- Left Parables.txt and AI-Parables.txt unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (sunroom mattress / stigma pierced by hospitality)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Mattress in the Sunroom** — retold from a Guardian first-person piece about a woman who visited a homeless heroin user in a psych ward, brought her home to sleep on a mattress in the sunroom, fed her porridge, and helped her into rehab.

- The mercy is not merely shelter. The deepest action is perceptual: she refuses the label and sees the wound beneath it.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it crosses toward someone many would have reduced to danger, disgust, or hopelessness, and does so without spectacle.
- It is distinct from the archive’s broader hospitality cluster: the key movement is not simply housing a traveler, but restoring personhood to someone who has already become socially invisible.
- The porridge / mattress details make it concrete enough to retell as a short parable without turning sentimental.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Cup of Jo comment about the mother feeding the overheated door-to-door salesman lunch** — morally lovely and very portable, but too thinly sourced as a single comment for this shelf.

- **Guideposts / Reddit-roundup style pieces** surfaced a few interesting moments, but their provenance or curation was too loose.

Heuristic sharpened this round:
- One of the cleanest modern parable shapes is this: a person refuses the story society is telling about someone, and by refusing it, opens a future.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (mercy that lays down hurry)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Woman Who Was Not in a Hurry** — retold from Stephanie Cole’s *My Unsung Hero* story, most fully accessible this round through the GPB write-up, where a customer answered a frightened teenage cashier’s freeze not with irritation but with the sentence, “Take your time. I’m not in a hurry.”

Why it cleared the bar:
- The mercy is precise and morally clean. Nothing material changes hands; the gift is the surrender of haste.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it names a common cruelty that often goes unconfessed: people can crush the weak simply by refusing to slow down.
- It is distinct from the archive’s other dignity stories. The nearest relatives involve shielding shame, restoring confidence, or refusing unfair gain; this one turns on laying down urgency so that another person can breathe and remain whole.
- The story also propagates well: the recipient spends years passing the same words on to others, which gives the act a seed-like quality instead of leaving it a one-off comfort.

- **Kate Elsley’s Story** — strong solidarity shape (an older man publicly holding an anti-Prop 8 sign on a train platform), but it felt a little more like witness than parable, and the political specificity narrowed the portability.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (spark of life / playful mercy in despair)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Race Down the Hall** — retold from Wil Davenport’s *My Unsung Hero* story as published by NHPR and Central Florida Public Media, about a therapist in an inpatient unit who did not try to reason him back to life, but tossed him a playful challenge: “I bet I’ll beat you to the community room.”

Why it cleared the bar:
- The mercy is very small and very exact. She does not overwhelm him with advice, diagnosis, or spectacle; she notices the last ember of agency and blows on it.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it is creative and proportionate: not every soul near death needs a lecture—sometimes it needs one light burden that can still be lifted.
- It is distinct from the archive’s other dignity stories. The closest relatives restore worth by praise, cover shame, or yield advantage; this one rekindles desire itself by offering a tiny purpose.
- The challenge is playful without being trivializing. That matters. It honors the man as still reachable, still answerable, still alive enough to race.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Susan Greenstein Prescott / Fred DeMayo** — strong and beautiful (“I liked listening to your voice”), but too close to existing archive lanes of teacherly blessing and truthful praise breaking shame.
- **The Guardian / gentle usher at the theatre** — humane, but too adjacent to the collection’s already-strong public-embarrassment comfort stories.
- **The Guardian / scary teens helping with crutches** — morally clean, but more reversal-of-expectation anecdote than compact parable-turn.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (forgiveness in deed / hallmate ride)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Door Across the Hall** — retold from Karen de Boer’s *My Unsung Hero* story, as carried most accessibly this round by NCPR, about the college hallmate she had repeatedly inconvenienced with noise who nevertheless drove her to a choir performance she had nearly missed.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The mercy comes from the injured party. That makes it morally sharper than ordinary help, because the gift is forgiveness enacted before it is discussed.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the one who had every right to say “now you will learn” instead says, in effect, “come, I know the way.”
- It is distinct from the archive’s nearby dignity stories. The closest relatives involve praise, comfort, or hidden provision; this one turns on pardon embodied in a ride.
- The story retells cleanly in under ten sentences and survives compression without sentimentality.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Patrick Furlong / guidance counselor tying the tie** — excellent and very moving; morally clean, but finally felt a little closer to the archive’s already-strong lane of dignifying recognition and timely reassurance.
- **Ashley Ludlow / ICU nurse’s phone call after her mother’s death** — exact and beautiful, but the archive already holds several grief-comfort stories that work in a closely related register.
- **Janet Kahn-Scolaro / hospital graduation** — lovely and inventive institutional mercy, but slightly more ceremonial than parabolic.
- **A / boss during psychosis** — morally serious and admirable; I may return to this lane, but this round the story felt more like crisis intervention than parable-turn.
- **Vivian Curren / “That was masterful”** — strong truthful praise after a public parenting trial, but too adjacent to existing archive entries where one sentence releases shame.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (grave-honor / hidden brain scan)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Man in the Suit at the Grave** — retold from Ashley Blas’s *My Unsung Hero* story, accessible this round through the Apple Podcasts episode description and the Hidden Brain archive page summary, about a driver who noticed grass covering part of her mother’s gravestone and dropped to his knees — still in his suit — to clear it away.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The mercy is exact. He does not comfort her with words about grief, nor outsource the task; he kneels and restores the name.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it joins humility to remembrance. The act is lowly, practical, and reverent all at once.
- It is distinct from the archive’s nearby grief stories. The closest relatives offer comfort to the mourner; this one honors the dead in order to serve the mourner.
- The visual image is unusually portable: a suited man kneeling in cemetery dirt so that another person’s love is not hidden under grass.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Brad Larsen / diner table** — morally beautiful and close to keepable; the bartender’s invitation to sit again at the last table with his mother is a strong grief-remembrance image, but this round it felt slightly thinner and more private than the grave story’s cleaner parable-turn.
- **Kate Elsley / sign on the train platform** — admirable solidarity with the socially shunned, but the political specificity narrowed the portability a bit for this shelf.
- **Tulika Prasad / stranger at the grocery store during autistic child’s meltdown** — promising moral shape, but the accessible detail this round stayed too thin; I could not trust a high-standard retelling from the summary alone.
- **Alexis Agnew / receptionist hug after irregular test results** — humane and exact, but too adjacent to the archive’s already-strong grief-and-tears comfort lane.
- **Stacia / Tom Sinclair / Laura Eshelman / A / Bethany Renfree / Lily** — good source family and worth having searched, but either too rescue-shaped, too summary-thin, or too close to existing archive forms.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (standing in another's empty place / fresh hidden brain lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Man Behind the Backstop** — retold from Olivia Joffrey’s *My Unsung Hero* story, most fully accessible this round through the KERA/NPR write-up, about a young man named Bob who showed up at a Father’s Day softball game so a sixth-grade girl whose father was lost to early Alzheimer’s and Parkinson’s would not have to stand alone.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The mercy is not rescue from danger but rescue from exclusion. That already makes it feel distinct inside the archive.
- It feels Jesus-grade because Bob does not merely sympathize with the girl’s loss; he steps into the empty place her sorrow had opened and bears it for a while.
- The act is clean and ego-light. He does not instruct, pity, or dramatize; he just arrives, joins the fathers, and lets her feel normal and accepted.
- The image travels well as parable: the backstop, the hill, the brown jacket, the empty place quietly filled.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Barbara Alvarez / hall minister at college move-in** — humane and strong, but too close to the archive’s existing lane of practical burden-sharing in a grief-saturated moment.
- **Virginia Squier / leather-clad stranger returning the child from the train** — morally clean and memorable, but it leans closer to a rescue anecdote and also rhymes a bit too directly with Good-Samaritan reversal-of-prejudice energy.

- **Kristi Reeves / Joann Moschella / Ian Sandler / Leahruth Jemilo / Molly Baker / Lilah Clevey** — compassionate and often moving, but either too close to existing grief-comfort / rescue lanes or too summary-thin to justify preservation over the stronger Olivia story.

## 2026-03-08 — web round (listening before judging / bully-ring broken by truth)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Voice in the Locker Room** — retold from Jennifer’s *My Unsung Hero* story, most fully accessible this round through the GPB/NPR write-up, about a bullied girl who expected fresh cruelty from a new ringleader and instead found someone who listened to the truth and stopped the torment.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The mercy is not softness from outside the situation, but a turn inside the structure of harm itself. Someone with social power chooses not to protect the ring, but to break it.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it begins with truthful hearing. The girl is saved not by argument, spectacle, or revenge, but because someone finally listens before judging.
- It is distinct from the archive’s nearby shame stories. Many existing entries cover embarrassment, grief, or provision; this one turns on repentance-like reversal in the very place where accusation was expected.
- The action is portable and clean in parable form: “I did not know; I will tell them to stop.”

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Betsy Cox / GPB** — very warm and humane; the stranger’s blessing over the exhausted mother and her child is memorable, but in the end it felt a touch more mood-lifting and sentimental than parable-sharp.
- **Aya McMillan / GPB** — exact and beautiful physical comfort during a frightening biopsy, but too close to the archive’s already-strong lane of grief / pain comfort through quiet presence and touch.
- **Heather Harper / GPB** — morally serious and strong (“Don’t ever let anyone tell you that you are grieving too long”), but still too adjacent to the archive’s grief-comfort family.
- **Sandra Bailey / Hidden Brain** and several other Hidden Brain summaries — promising, but I did not get enough concrete, trustworthy detail this round to justify a high-confidence retelling.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (anonymous table / dignity before speech)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Table Set at Noon** — retold from WBUR’s *Kind World* story **“The Little Giant”** about Curtis Bishop, who was homeless and scavenging leftovers in an Ottawa food court until an unknown office worker began quietly setting out a fresh hot meal for him each Tuesday as though for an expected guest.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The mercy is not mere feeding but re-humanizing. The stranger refuses to let the man receive food in the form that shame had assigned him.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the kindness is structured around dignity: a wiped table, utensils laid out, water set there, no speech to display virtue.
- The moral turn is rare and sharp: the recipient’s soul changes not because he is persuaded, but because someone he respects treats him as worth serving.
- The weekly silence helps; it keeps the act from becoming patronage or therapy and makes it closer to sacrament.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Qingdao bus driver / muddied construction worker** — strong public restoration of dignity, and the line “No shame in hard work” is excellent, but the source chain felt too viral-fragmentary for the archive’s standard.
- **KindSpring: Quiet Dignity at a Truck Stop** — morally clean, especially the disguised gift through the waitress, but too close to older provision-without-exposure patterns already in the collection.
- **KindSpring: Don’t We All?** — concise and wise, though it lands more as aphoristic reversal than as a fully embodied parable scene.
- **Kind World: A Second Mother to Me** and **Remembering Karim** — deeply humane, but either too adjacent to existing grief-adoption lanes or too diffuse across many acts to justify preservation over the sharper food-court story.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (humiliation answered with warmth)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Cookies Across the Partition** — retold from Julie Ort’s *My Unsung Hero* story, using the TPR/NPR transcript for the concrete details: after a humiliating fall in a college physics class, she hid in a library carrel, and an unknown classmate slid two warm cookies across the partition and said only, “I was in physics class today.”

Why it cleared the bar:
- The helper neither pretends not to have seen the humiliation nor turns it into a discussion. He acknowledges it just enough to place warmth beside it.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the act is both incarnate and restrained: food, timing, silence, no extraction of thanks, no advice.
- The cookies matter morally because they are not solution or sermon, only fellowship small enough to be received by someone who cannot bear another word.
- Distinctive shape for the archive: not rescue, not provision for survival, not public advocacy, but the healing of acute shame through tactful presence.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Barbara Alvarez / dorm move-in / hall minister Jody** — excellent and true, but too adjacent to the archive’s existing grief-accompaniment lane.
- **Connie Sherburne / “Let them” insurance agent** — very wise and possibly archive-worthy in another collection of sayings, but here it felt more like counsel than a parable scene.
- **Leahruth Jemilo / toll collector “I got you, girl”** — lovely, but the action stayed largely verbal; it did not carry the same embodied turn as the cookies story.
- **StoryCorps Fellow Travelers** — promising, but this round I did not see enough concrete detail without going deeper into transcript/audio than felt necessary once the stronger keeper had surfaced.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (costly promise / neighbor adoption lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Promise Across the Hall** — retold from StoryCorps’ **A Neighbor’s Promise — A Blended Family Remembers Their Journey**. A single mother in housing precarity promises a dying neighbor that she will take his three children if he dies, and then keeps that promise until the children can call her “Mom” without dividing the word.

Why it cleared the bar:
- It is costly in a way that cannot be sentimentalized: not a ride, a meal, a kind sentence, or a discreet rescue, but years of table-space, rule-making, conflict-bearing, and shared bread.
- The moral turn is unusually clean. She does not merely feel for the children or advocate from a distance; she makes herself family to them.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it receives the orphan not as a project but as kin, and it honors a promise made in the shadow of death.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: covenantal mercy across a hallway; adoption-like neighbor-love rather than stranger aid or public-shame covering.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Guardian opera usher** — beautiful and tactful, but too adjacent to the archive’s already rich shame-covering / giving-someone-a-place-to-recover-their-face lane.
- **Guardian San José airport departure tax** — morally clean, but too slight compared with the heavier promise-keeping candidate.
- **Guardian train worker / airport carousel / flight-attendant leads** — humane, but they did not separate enough from existing accompaniment and rescue families in the archive.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (education mercy / future-protecting authority)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Principal Who Would Not Let Him Leave** — retold from Jon Hegwood’s *My Unsung Hero* story about principal Billy Barnett. A 16-year-old father with two jobs comes to leave school, and the principal refuses to baptize his exhaustion as wisdom; instead he insists on graduation and reshapes the schedule so the boy can finish.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is not soft encouragement alone. Barnett tells the truth against immediate pressure: today’s burdens are real, but they must not be allowed to steal tomorrow’s doors.
- He also bears cost concretely by changing the boy’s schedule. This keeps the story from becoming merely advice; it is authority turned into accommodation for the sake of another’s future.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it joins firmness and mercy: he does not flatter sacrifice, yet he bends the institution around the burdened person rather than making the burdened person disappear.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: future-guarding mercy from a local authority figure; not rescue, not comfort, not secret financial provision, but a refusal to let hardship prematurely close a life.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Donald Kabara / metro attendant sending the son back for one more hug** — very clean and memorable, but still a touch slighter than the principal story’s future-shaping weight.
- **Eleanor Heginbotham / post office car breakdown** — lovely communal action and tea afterward, but too adjacent to the archive’s existing help-in-transit cluster.
- **Caitlin Shetterly / “Most people are good”** — beautiful sentence, and perhaps keeper-grade in a sayings collection, but here the embodied act remained too small.
- **Ellen Wyoming DeLoy / train conductor danger** — promising, but the accessible detail this round stayed too thin to retell with confidence.

AI-Parables.txt left unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (dignity-at-the-table / homeless guest lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Seat at the Party** — retold from the StoryCorps David Wright / Eddie Lanier story. A man who has been giving small roadside help refuses to keep mercy at arm’s length on New Year’s Eve; he brings the homeless man home, trusts his wife to receive him, gives him a shower and clean clothes, and lets him be known at the table.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is not almsgiving alone but restored personhood. The mercy crosses a threshold and reverses social distance.
- The line "there was a lot more to me than you might imagine" gives the story unusual clarity: the act does not merely meet need; it reveals the hidden self beneath public ruin.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it turns a stigmatized figure from object-of-charity into welcomed guest, and does so on a feast night when exclusion could have been easiest.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: table fellowship with the cast-off; not just feeding, not merely emergency rescue, but hospitality that restores face.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (receiving-help / second-armful lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Woman Who Carried the Second Armful** — retold from Brian Perkins’ *My Unsung Hero* story. A woman at the dry cleaners sees a man with a cane insisting he can manage alone, carries the rest of his clothes to the truck anyway, and says the line that makes the story keeper-grade: **“You’ve been helping people all your life. Now it’s your turn. Accept it.”**

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is not ordinary niceness but a clean rebuke to pride disguised as self-sufficiency. The mercy teaches a helper how to become a receiver.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it restores humility without humiliation; the woman neither pities him nor argues at length, but quietly completes what his body cannot carry.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: receiving mercy without shame. The shelf has many strong stories about stepping in; this one teaches the other half of love.
- The line is unusually memorable without being sentimental, and the scene is concrete enough to retell faithfully.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **Megan Atherton / Toni Cranston** — excellent and morally real, but too close to the archive’s already substantial shelter / take-you-home / stranger-gives-you-a-place-to-stay lane.
- **Emma Carlson Berne** — beautiful act of seeing an overwhelmed mother and her older children, but closer to affirmation/blessing than to a sharply new parable turn for this collection.
- **Guardian theatre usher** — still strong; still adjacent to our existing shame-covering / making-a-new-place-inside-the-house cluster.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (forgiveness / second-chance lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Man Who Thought of His Own Son** — retold from the StoryCorps Darius Clark Monroe / David Ned story. A teenager robs a bank, later seeks out one of the men he terrified, and is met not only with forgiveness but with active blessing because the victim imagines his own son someday needing the same mercy.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is unusually clean: the harmed person does not merely let go of hatred in private; he rehumanizes the offender by placing him inside the horizon of fatherly hope.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the mercy does not deny fear or wrong. The victim names the terror, yet refuses to let terror have the final word.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: second-chance mercy from the wronged party toward the wrongdoer. This is meaningfully different from our rescue, dignity, grief-comfort, and quiet-service clusters.
- The line about thinking of his own son gives the story its depth: forgiveness becomes imaginable not through abstraction but through love for one’s own child.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **A False Witness and the Man He Put in Prison for Decades** — extraordinary and keeper-grade in a broader forgiveness collection, but heavier, more historically layered, and less parable-clean for this shelf than the bank-forgiveness story.
- **How a Teacher’s Act of Kindness Bound Her Student and Family Together** — beautiful, but too near our existing teacher / protected-future / belonging lane.
- **Rogelio Martinez and Lisa Moya King** — morally strong foster-care / refuge shape, but too adjacent to our adoption and shelter cluster.
- **Paul Braun and “Philip”** — moving mutual gratitude, though less sharply parable-shaped in the accessible detail.

AI-Parables.txt left unchanged this round.


## 2026-03-09 — web round (fresh transcript lanes / hidden generosity preserving dignity)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Teacher Who Called It a Scholarship** — retold from Silvana Clark’s *My Unsung Hero* story after checking the fuller Podscan transcript. Midway through college, Silvana could not afford another semester; her drama teacher quietly paid tuition and room and board, but told her she had won a scholarship.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is not only educational rescue; it is mercy hidden inside dignity. He helps her without forcing her to carry the posture of being singled out for charity.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the teacher protects both future and face. He does not merely keep her in school; he makes the gift light enough for her to receive.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: concealed generosity that preserves standing. This is meaningfully different from the principal story, which is about insisting on a future and reshaping the path; this one is about giving under a name the recipient can bear.
- The line from the office — “there is no scholarship” — gives the story its clean reveal and makes the moral structure unusually crisp.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Stephanie Cole’s story** — strong and true, but closer to everyday gentleness than to a rare parable-turn.
- **Joann Moschella’s lavender bunny-suit rescuer** — delightful and humane, but more whimsical than keeper-grade for this shelf.
- **Barbara Alvarez’s move-in-day helper** — morally real, but still adjacent to the archive’s grief-accompaniment and logistics-help cluster.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (school-lunch / public-shame lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Man Who Changed the Lunch Line** — retold from the StoryCorps Kenny Thompson / Gary Barber / Dakota Gibson story. Children who could not pay were publicly marked with a cold cheese sandwich; Kenny first paid the balances, then pushed until the policy changed so poor children were no longer singled out in the line.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is sharper than simple generosity: the mercy does not only fill empty stomachs, it removes the public mechanism of shame.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it protects little ones and refuses to let poverty become a spectacle in front of their peers.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: changing the humiliating rule itself, not merely relieving one instance of need.
- The image is concrete and memorable without melodrama: a lunch line, a cold sandwich, a man who cannot bear what the children are being taught about themselves.

Considered but did NOT keep:
- **“Call Lil:” A Mother Remembers Her Life, Love and Legacy** — beautiful fidelity and strength, but not parable-shaped for this shelf.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (neighbor-through-the-night / repentance-into-presence lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Neighbor Who Came Because Once He Did Not** — retold from Patricia Nieshoff’s *My Unsung Hero* story via GPB. In the night of her son’s seizure, a neighbor follows the ambulance by another road: he secures her open house, gathers the things panic left behind, brings the child’s stuffed animal, finds the right hospital, and stays. The line that made it keeper-grade is his reason: **“Long ago, I was not there for someone ... if there was a chance to be there for someone in the future, then I would do it.”**

Why it cleared the bar:
- The mercy is more than crisis competence. It is repentance transfigured into availability. An old failure becomes the seed of present faithfulness.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it does not advertise remorse or wallow in it; it simply appears at the hospital door with what love would have remembered to bring.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: past regret becoming future readiness. We have rescue, dignity, hidden generosity, and public-shame-removal stories; this one teaches how remembered failure can ripen into mercy.
- The details are concrete and parable-clean: open door, keys and wallet forgotten, stuffed animal retrieved, hospitals called, night watched through.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Ashley Blas / cemetery driver** — beautiful reverence and strong dignity-of-grief shape, but narrower than the Patricia story’s deeper moral turn.
- **Joy Diaz / “Buy a house”** — major generosity and real consequence across generations, but less parable-clean for this shelf because the scale of the gift can overshadow the moral pattern.
- **Clara Temple / cafeteria worker** — lovely and specific, but too adjacent to our existing school-lunch and shame-in-public lane.
- **Tulika Prasad / grocery-store stranger** and **A’s Story / boss securing psychiatric help** remain promising, but the publicly accessible detail I found this round stayed too thin for a confident retelling.

AI-Parables.txt left unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (bedside ceremony / carried honor lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Graduation Brought to the Bedside** — retold from Janet Kahn-Scolaro’s *My Unsung Hero* story via GPB. A dying mother could not attend her daughter’s high school graduation, so hospital staff and friends recreated the ceremony in the hospital room, complete with cap and gown, diploma, and a reception next door.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The mercy does not only comfort the suffering; it carries public joy to the place where weakness would otherwise exclude a mother from her daughter’s moment.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the helpers refuse the logic that says celebration belongs only to the strong enough to reach it. They bear the feast to the failing body.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: honor brought to the bedside; not rescue, not shame-covering, not forgiveness, but ceremonial joy moved toward the one who cannot rise.
- The image is concrete and unusually clean: cap and gown in a hospital room, diploma beside a deathbed, refreshments in the next room “just as it would be” at an ordinary celebration.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Tulika Prasad / grocery-store stranger** — much stronger than on earlier passes once the GPB writeup supplied detail. The woman’s help is beautiful: unloading the cart, paying, walking the mother out, and shielding her from the crowd’s gaze without advice or pity. Still, I left it off this round because it remains closer to our already-rich shame-covering / nonjudgment lane than the bedside-graduation story.
- **A’s Story / boss securing psychiatric care** — morally real and possibly keeper-grade in another round, but still too summary-thin in publicly accessible detail for a confident retelling.
- **GPB page-3 roundup** — useful discovery page, but not itself a retellable story.

## 2026-03-09 — web round (hidden-no-more / funeral-home lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Woman Who Would Not Let the Hidden Die Alone** — retold from the GPB / *My Unsung Hero* story about Mas Masumoto and funeral-home worker Renée Johnson. When a disabled aunt long hidden away as a state ward seemed to have no family, Johnson searched old records until she found her relatives, so the woman would not die alone or remain erased by the family’s old shame.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is not simple kindness to the bereaved; it is a refusal to let invisibility have the final word.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it seeks the one who had been quietly removed from sight and restores her to kinship before death.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: hidden person returned to family; shame interrupted not by speech alone, but by painstaking, unnecessary labor.
- The image is parable-clean: old census records, a phone call, a family sitting down in disbelief, an aunt restored from presumed death to living presence.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **River Adams / Roy Rogers manager Ed** — deeply good and concrete, but still too close to our food-and-dignity / hunger-relief lane.
- **JoAnne Foley / Nancy Allspach** — beautiful tenderness toward a dying infant, but adjacent to our existing bedside / nurse-compassion cluster.
- **Jeff Balch / the trash collectors** — morally real and memorable, though closer to acknowledgment-of-grief than to a rarer new moral structure.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (mercy-without-humiliation / would-be-thief lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Supper After the Purse** — retold from Langston Hughes’s *Thank You, Ma’am*. A boy tries to snatch a woman’s purse; she catches him, sees the neglect under the offense, brings him home, makes him wash, leaves the door open and the purse in sight, feeds him, and finally gives him the money he had tried to steal.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral action is not mere leniency. It is correction without humiliation.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the woman does not deny the wrong, but she refuses to let the boy be reduced to the wrong.
- The strongest turn is the open door and the visible purse: mercy creates a chance to become trustworthy by first being trusted.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: not hidden charity alone, not forgiveness after years, not alms to a stranger, but the immediate rehumanizing of someone in the act of failing.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **The Thief Who Became a Disciple** (Zen / Shichiri Kojun) — very strong, but this shelf is already rich in thief-met-by-mercy stories, and this pass’s stronger new distinctiveness came from the no-humiliation / motherly-correction lane.
- **How a Teacher’s Act of Kindness Bound her Student and Family Together** (StoryCorps / Cecile Doyle) — deeply tender and memorable, but more testimony than parable-shaped turn.
- **He Survived the Holocaust Because of a Stranger’s Kindness** (StoryCorps / Philip Lazowski) — morally luminous, especially “Hold on to my dress,” but the historical extremity and providential ending made me leave it off this round rather than risk forcing it into the archive’s parable cadence.
- **The teacher-and-stolen-watch internet story** — moving, but provenance still feels too thin / chain-forward for a keeper-grade addition.

AI-Parables.txt left unchanged this round.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (lavish-grace / hidden-artist / quiet-mercy comparison lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Feast She Poured Out** — retold from Karen Blixen / Isak Dinesen’s *Babette’s Feast*. A refugee cook who has quietly sustained a small religious household wins a fortune and spends the whole of it on one extravagant meal, and the feast loosens old grievances and scruples without ever arguing with them.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral action is not mere charity but costly abundance offered where people have grown afraid of joy.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the transformation happens through gift, table, and reconciliation rather than through pressure or moral performance.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: beauty as mercy; a feast that becomes a kind of amnesty.
- Strong concrete image set: hidden greatness in a servant, suspicious saints at table, old bitterness thawing course by course, the whole fortune poured out in a single act.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Guardian / Joanne saved my life** — very strong hospitality and rescue, but it stayed more testimonial than parable-clean on this pass.
- **Guardian / the lifeguard with the towel** — beautiful dignity-protecting mercy; still somewhat too slight in public detail for a keeper over Babette.
- **Guardian / the theatre usher** — tender and true, but narrower in moral range.
- **Guardian / the mother whose car was hit and who said “don’t worry”** — real mercy, but too brief for this shelf’s higher bar.

- **Basheer / Oru Manushyan** — still interesting because a thief becomes the humane one, but this archive already has a rich thief-met-by-mercy cluster.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (trust-before-proof / prisoner-returns lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Prisoner Who Came Back With a Haircut** — retold from Wanda Bullard’s *Small Town Prisoner* (The Moth). An elderly desk officer in a tiny town trusts a jailed young man enough to release him to fetch bail from his father, and the young man returns late only because he stopped first for the haircut the old man had advised.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is not leniency alone but trust offered before proof.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the old man sees a recoverable son where the system sees a manageable offender.
- The strongest image is the transformed return: the room full of policemen does not recognize the man they expected to deceive them.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: repentance summoned by unguarded trust rather than by punishment, lecture, or public shame.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **The Last Leaf** — searched fresh comparison pages, but the archive already has two strong leaf-in-the-storm entries; no reason to add another coat of paint.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (shame-refused / father-who-sees-clearly lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Father Who Said, Do Not Sneak** — retold from StoryCorps’s *The Saint of Dry Creek* / Patrick Haggerty story. A farm father recognizes what his son is trying to hide and, instead of humiliating him, warns him not to learn the soul-warping habit of sneaking through life as though his deepest affections were shameful.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is unusually precise: the father does not merely accept; he actively refuses to let shame become the child’s tutor.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the mercy is truthful rather than indulgent. Nothing is blurred, but neither is the boy crushed.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: not rescue after collapse, not forgiveness after injury, but a parent preventing inner self-contempt before it hardens into a life.
- The images are parable-clean: glitter before an assembly, cow-stained jeans, the hidden son in the hallway, and the father’s simple command not to sneak.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **The Door She Opened** — morally beautiful hidden refuge, and perhaps worth revisiting; I left it off because this pass’s stronger form was not secret shelter but shame directly interrupted by truthful love.
- **The Bookmobile** — genuinely luminous; it teaches hope through knowledge, but its movement is more awakening than moral confrontation.
- **Lanvester Jackson / Mary Frances Early** — lovely and true, yet still too near the archive’s already strong teacher-saved-my-future lane.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (mother-made-room / enemy-made-kin lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Mother Who Made Room for Another Son** — retold from StoryCorps’s Mary Johnson / Oshea Israel conversation. After her only son was murdered, Mary eventually went to prison to meet the young man who killed him, forgave him, and over time received him into ordinary mother-son nearness, even living next door.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is not forgiveness as a feeling but forgiveness embodied as kinship.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the woman does not deny the gravity of the evil, yet she refuses to let death have two sons instead of one.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: enemy made kin; mercy moved out of ceremony and into daily neighborhood life.
- Strong concrete image set: prison visit, the embrace, “my second son,” and the holiness of trash day / ordinary check-ins after the impossible has happened.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:

## 2026-03-16 — web round (amnesty-day / mercy-before-verdict lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Father Who Kept an Amnesty Day** — retold from StoryCorps’s *Amnesty Days: One Father’s Made-Up Day of Forgiveness*. A father, remembering how guilt once made him furtive and sick, creates a recurring day on which his children may confess anything without punishment or lecture.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is precise and unusually durable: mercy is arranged *before* confession, so truth does not have to fight its way through fear.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it opposes concealment without cheapening wrongdoing; the father is not indifferent to damage, but he values an unhidden child more than an intact table.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: not enemy-love, not rescue, not teacherly intervention, but household mercy that teaches honesty by making it safe.
- Strong concrete image set: smashed porch glass, a scorched toilet seat painted with watercolors, a boy unable to eat under the weight of secrecy, and a father who would rather lose furniture than train a child in hiding.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **The Facebook Post That Led To A Kidney Donation** — beautiful and costly generosity, but the moral action remained more exemplary than parable-sharp in this pass.
- **It Feels Like a Gift: How Taking a Name Kept One Man’s Legacy Alive** — moving and memorable, but it belongs more to remembrance and tribute than to this collection’s clearest mercy/kingdom lane.
- **Longtime Friends Reconnect in a Homeless Shelter** — warm and real, yet too diffuse in moral turn for this archive’s standard.
- **Greg Gibson and Wayne Lo / Ronald Simpson-Bey** — morally serious restorative-justice material, but too near the archive’s already-strong enemy-made-kin and murderer-forgiven lane.

After an estranged former husband dies, the first wife not only forgives him at the bedside but sees the grief of the later beloved, seats her with the children at the funeral, and asks her to remain part of the family.

- Distinctive lane for the archive: not enemy made kin after violence, but rival made kin through grief, forgiveness, and table fellowship.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (ask-before-you-heal / false-pity-corrected lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Man Who Said, Ask Before You Heal** — retold from Joshua Arnold’s *The Altar Call* (The Moth transcript / Love Hurts SLAM page reviewed). In a church service, a man in a wheelchair is publicly singled out for healing prayer by people who never ask what help he actually wants; when handed a microphone, he stops the scene and tells them they saw the chair and assumed the need.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is distinct and unusually clean: the story rebukes not overt cruelty but the softer violence of presuming another person’s wound.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it aligns with the Teacher’s own habit of asking, even where the crowd thinks the need is obvious.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: false pity corrected by truthful dignity; mercy must listen before it names what is broken.
- Strong concrete image set: the aisle, the imposed hands, the microphone, and the sentence that stops the whole room — “You saw my chair and thought you had seen my need.”

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Maryann Jacobi Gray / It Hurts To Hurt Someone** — morally rich, especially the neighboring woman who would not let the driver wait alone, but the strongest movement was explanatory rather than parable-shaped.
- **A Fire Took Everything, But She Gained a Friend for Life** — warm and admirable, but it stayed in the guardian-angel / long-term mentor lane without a sharper kingdom turn than the archive already has.
- **Jerry Mitchell / Reconciling the Past** — powerful public repentance and forgiveness, yet too near the archive’s already deep murderer-forgiven / enemy-made-kin cluster.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (mother-took-up-the-comb / mercy-before-words lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Mother Who Took Up the Comb** — retold from Betty Thompson's StoryCorps account. As a pregnant teenager, she expected only devastation; instead, after naming the truth, her mother sat her down and combed her hair, letting touch carry forgiveness before words knew how.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is unusually clean and concrete: mercy does not deny the wrong, but neither does it let shame become the final teacher.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the mother answers exposure not with indulgence or harshness, but with a restoring tenderness that keeps the daughter inside love while the truth is still hard.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: mercy before explanation; forgiveness communicated through embodied care before speech can manage it.
- Strong concrete image set: the floor not opening, the chair, the comb, and the hands saying what the mouth cannot yet say.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Monica Woo / Arctic Rifts** — rich and moving recovery of love toward a difficult dead brother, but the shape remained more memoir-of-reconciliation than parable-clean for this shelf.
- **Mary Amato / what you're doing is so valuable** — morally lovely release from vocational shame, but too adjacent to existing quiet-dignity / public-shame-lifted entries.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (friend-pulled-him-aside / private-correction lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Friend Who Pulled Him Aside** — retold from Ritch Addison’s My Unsung Hero story. What cleared the bar was not rescue or consolation but private correction that saves a frightened boy from building his whole self out of mockery.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is unusually clean: a friend tells hard truth without humiliating him, and the rebuke becomes a mercy rather than a public victory.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it joins truth to dignity; it does not excuse the harm, but it addresses it where repentance can still bloom.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: private correction before cruelty hardens into character.
- Strong concrete image set: the cutting joke, the friend taking him aside, the delayed self-recognition, and the weapon of wit being laid down.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Lilah Clevey / other driver after the crash** — morally beautiful, but too close to `The Scratch She Chose to Forget`, which already holds the accident-forgiveness lane with greater compression.
- **Ed the fast-food manager feeding the refugee family** — clean and generous, but too adjacent to existing hidden-feeding / open-hand-to-the-hungry entries.
- **Sister Janice visiting James Martin’s father** — deeply good ministry of presence, but not distinct enough from the archive’s existing presence-at-the-bedside cluster.
- **Shalom Auslander / grandmother and the forbidden gum** — vivid anti-legalist energy, but too morally mixed for this shelf because the kindness depends on furtiveness and defiance without enough clear love toward the mother.
- **Jamil Zaki / doctor who pulled up a chair** and **Rasha Kowalewski / mi amor nurse** remained strong, but both overlap lanes the archive already has in fuller form.

## 2026-03-16 — web round (beggar-saw-her-sorrow / poor-comforts-the-harsh lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Beggar Who Saw Her Sorrow** — retold from Laura Eshelman’s *My Unsung Hero* story. A woman in a mental-health spiral snaps at a man asking for change, then turns back in repentance; when she apologizes, the man she has treated as a nuisance takes her hand and comforts her instead.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral inversion is unusually clean: the one asking for alms becomes the giver, and the one who imagined herself superior discovers that she is the one in need.
- It feels Jesus-grade because it joins repentance to mercy without sentimentality. The woman does turn back, but the deeper wonder is that the poor man answers insult with blessing rather than humiliation.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: the despised person becoming the comforter of the one who despised him.
- Strong concrete image set: the street corner, the forgotten coins at the bottom of the pocket, the rough hands, and the sentence of peace.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Heather Harper / the woman in church who named long grief** — morally strong and memorable, but it stayed closer to grief-validation than to a sharper new parable turn for this shelf.
- **Susan Greenstein Prescott / the teacher who said he liked her voice** — tender and quietly future-making, yet finally more encouragement than kingdom-shaped inversion.
- **Natalie Cook / little boy at the playground** — beautiful inclusion, especially “what she could do, not what she couldn’t,” but it leaned heartwarming more than revelatory beside this round’s keeper.
- **Teresa Hernandez / nurse during preterm labor** and **Calvin Lowe / “your son is my son”** remained good stories, but both overlap care-in-crisis lanes the archive already holds in stronger form.
- **Nora Durkin / hotline operator** and **Leahruth Jemilo / toll collector** were humane and clean, but neither produced as portable or surprising a parable image as the corner-beggar story.

AI-Parables.txt left unchanged this round.


## 2026-03-16 — web round (firm-let-him-keep-working / dignity-beyond-usefulness lane)
Kept and added to Parables.txt:
- **The Firm That Let Him Keep Working** — retold from Paul Cotter’s *My Unsung Hero* account. After early-onset Alzheimer’s cost his father one job after another and people mistook cognitive decline for weakness of character, a small local firm knowingly hired him for modest tasks, paid him, respected him, and later let him go with grief and a farewell party when it was no longer safe for him to stay.

Why it cleared the bar:
- The moral turn is unusually clean: mercy is shown not by sentiment but by preserving a man’s dignity when his usefulness is visibly diminishing.
- It feels Jesus-grade because the firm refuses the world’s cruel arithmetic; they do not ask only what the man can still produce, but whether he may still be treated as one of them.
- Distinctive lane for the archive: honor guarded in decline; bread and belonging given to the failing without humiliation.
- Strong concrete image set: the keys handed back in shame, the simple copy work, the paycheck, the late chapter, and the goodbye party.

Considered seriously but did NOT keep:
- **Claire Burnside Och / cork in the wine** — graceful downward power, but finally too slight beside the archive’s strongest shame-lifted entries.
- **Caitlin Shetterly / most people are good** — memorable and clean, yet more proverb-like than parable-bearing in this collection’s terms.
- **Ashley Blas / driver clearing the gravestone** — tender and vivid, but too adjacent to existing grief-attended / stranger-honors-the-dead lanes.
- **Tulika Prasad / stranger helping with autistic son in the store** — likely humane and perceptive, but the available detail stayed too thin to justify a keeper.
- **Mark Metersky / manipulative patient surprises medical student** and **Clarice / safe space for joy on the darkest day** remained promising but did not yield enough concrete shape from the accessible sources to clear the bar.
